We should do a video about them  someday!" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ghq9EwiXbRbWSnDzF/fhkvrvtrjozqvmcdzjkh" /><figcaption>From a Kurzgesagt <a href="https://x.com/Kurz_Gesagt/status/1142113204778221568">tweet</a></figcaption></figure></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbt0rju2o0m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbt0rju2o0m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>LPTs are heavy and intransportable enough as they are: providing more saturation margin than 0.2 teslas would be expensive.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp4x91s0syz8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp4x91s0syz8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Damage to the kraft paper and machine oil substance that a transformer tank is filled to the brim with can be measured by taking a sample and establishing how much dissolved gas it contains. This directly correlates with how much chemical decomposition was caused by a solar storm.&nbsp;</p><p>Finding elevated concentrations of hydrogen means "I'm experiencing partial discharge." Acetylene means "something is arcing inside me, this is bad." Rising carbon monoxide means "my paper insulation is baking."<br /><br />For example, here's what South African engineers found in one of their transformers (graph found in <a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/research/coming-down-to-earth-what-if-a-big-geomagnetic-storm-does-hit/">a previous solar storms risk breakdown</a> by Opefficient Givanthropy):</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ghq9EwiXbRbWSnDzF/sxt9yaxudnvs5bbj5db5" /></figure></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngkwm6s7d9lf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgkwm6s7d9lf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Costs from transformer damage in the US due to common low-level solar storms is <a href="https://centerforsecuritypolicy.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/06/Appendix-F-2015Zurich-ElectricalClaimsandSpaceWeather.pdf">estimated</a> at 10 billion dollars a year. (Same for the EU.) Meanwhile, IP theft from China <a href="https://www.nbr.org/wp-content/uploads/pdfs/publications/IP_Commission_Report_Update.pdf">costs upwards of 225 billion</a> to Americans every year; we remain quite the positive externality!&nbsp;</p><p>This is frustrating to me, because Emprimus is having trouble finding customers, and the reason their device costs half a million in the first place is because the only people who are scaling it paid them nothing for the 10 years and millions in R&amp;D that went behind the design.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4zszabj1ubu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4zszabj1ubu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>If utilities weren't so f***ing secretive about things, I could get Claude Opus 4.6 to run simulations of grid failures in every country in the world to estimate which transformers are the most vulnerable, how exactly the grid would fail, etc. This is what New Zealand did successfully! And it's well within current AI capabilities.&nbsp;<br /><br />But as with a lot of claims about AI capabilities—cancer cure, etc.—it's bottlenecked by data.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngo9zqok3elo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgo9zqok3elo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The <a href="https://www.undrr.org/news/texas-coldwave-disaster-how-cascading-risks-took-out-entire-power-grid">winter storm that hit Texas</a> in 2021 knocked out half of energy generation capacity at its peak and caused a third of Austin's sewage lift stations to go offline, causing overflows of sewage in the streets and, far worse, into waterways later used by treatment water plants unable to do their job without power, thereby injecting sewage into tap water and putting 17 million people under boil-water advisories for weeks. (I don't even—what—designing sewerage systems that don't do that is a different post.)&nbsp;<br /><br />Texas estimates 80-130 billion dollars in damages and at least 250 dead—from a state with 30 million people in it.&nbsp;</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ghq9EwiXbRbWSnDzF/solar-storms#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The current SOTA model was released without safety evals](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9woCWqiDbSFPXpxvn/the-current-sota-model-was-released-without-safety-evals)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><strong>TL;DR:&nbsp;</strong>OpenAI released GPT-5.4 Thinking and GPT-5.4 Pro on March 5, 2026.&nbsp;<strong>GPT-5.4 Pro is likely the best model in the world for many catastrophic risk-relevant tasks</strong>, including biological research R&amp;D, orchestrating cyberoffense operations, and computer use. It has&nbsp;<strong>no system card</strong>, and, to our best knowledge, has been released without any safety evals. We argue this has occurred at least once before, with GPT-5.2 Pro, and provide recommendations for how a team could conduct fast, independent risk assessments of models post-deployment.&nbsp;<br /><br /><strong>IMPORTANT EDIT: </strong>This problem, where Pro models don't have a safety card, has existed since at least o3-pro. Others have noticed this issue before (for o3 and GPT-5). Additionally, Pro "models" are <i>probably</i> just fancy scaffolding that leverage test-time compute on top of the Thinking models. However, we think our recommendations still stand, because:&nbsp;</p><ol><li>The best system (e.g., model + leveraging parallel test-time compute) has meaningfully greater capabilities in catastrophic risk-relevant areas than just the model. In this case, the model developer clearly thinks so because of its much greater price, because it is listed as a separate model in the API, and because of the way that it is framed in the model release announcement.&nbsp;</li><li>There exist difficult-to-externally-verify claims from OAI about when it has a (soft) commitment to releasing Pro models' benchmark scores, meaning the safety community should probably still look for these things in future models.&nbsp;</li><li>There are many other frontier and open-source labs for which the pre-deployment public evaluations are wholly insufficient to meaningfully assess risk.&nbsp;</li></ol><p>We should have been more aggressive about looking into what Pro models actually are, and for others' previous comments on this topic. See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9woCWqiDbSFPXpxvn?commentId=FgShmxK2Yh4ThGbsb">this comment thread</a> for more - thanks to loops who pointed this out :)</p><h1>GPT-5.4 Pro is really good</h1><p>OpenAI&nbsp;<a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-5-4/"><u>released</u></a> both GPT-5.4 Thinking (what people usually mean when they say GPT-5.4) and GPT-5.4 Pro<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4m5zkf7jrsx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4m5zkf7jrsx">[1]</a></sup></span>, the latter of which is designed for “people who want maximum performance on complex tasks.” GPT-5.4 Pro is extremely expensive, and takes a very long time to complete a task. However, it is likely the best model in the world in several areas, including expert-level Q&amp;A and browser use. Alongside the release announcement, OpenAI presented GPT-5.4 Pro’s performance on a subset of capability benchmarks. Here’s a comparison of benchmark scores across the top three frontier models; we only report scores if they were in all models’ system cards<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreff05a4nkqygr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnf05a4nkqygr">[2]</a></sup></span>:</p><p>&nbsp;</p><figure class="table"><table style="border-style: none;"><tbody><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>Benchmark</strong></p></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>Gemini 3.1 Pro</strong></p></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>GPT-5.4 Pro</strong></p></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>Opus 4.6</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">GPQA Diamond</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">94.3%</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>94.4%</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">91.3%</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">HLE (no tools)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>44.4%</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">42.7%</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">40.0%</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">HLE (with tools)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">51.4%</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>58.7%</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">53.1%</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">ARC-AGI-2 (Verified)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">77.1%</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>83.3%</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">68.8%</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">BrowseComp</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">85.9%</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>89.3%</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">84.0%</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>Based on these results, we expect GPT-5.4 Pro to be SOTA at the Virology Capabilities Test, Agentic-Bio Capabilities Benchmark, FrontierMath, and anywhere else depending on academic reasoning, a broad knowledge base, and that scales nicely with inference compute. BrowseComp and SOTA on FinanceAgent v1.1 (61.5%) make us think it’s probably also SOTA at automating office work generally.&nbsp;</p><p>The biggest hole in saying that it’s overall SOTA is agentic coding, but given SOTA measures of abstract reasoning through ARC-AGI-2, we think it’s likely that,&nbsp;<a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/evidence-for-inference-scaling-in-ai-cyber-tasks-increased-evaluation-budgets-reveal-higher-success-rates"><u>given enough compute</u></a>, it would beat Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.1 Pro on things like SWE-Bench and Terminal-Bench 2.0.&nbsp;</p><p>Yet, it was released without any public safety evals. The&nbsp;<a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-4-thinking/introduction"><u>system card published alongside the release</u></a> is only for GPT-5.4 Thinking. It’s possible that GPT-5.4 Pro was tested for safety properties internally (We would hope at least something like Petri was run to make sure there wasn’t a crazy distribution shift?), but we were unable to find any public information about this, if true. We would bet significant money that OAI did not run a suite of internal evals at least as comprehensive as those in the GPT-5.4 Thinking model card prior to Pro’s release.&nbsp;</p><p>It is highly unlikely that GPT-5.4 Pro poses catastrophic misuse or misalignment risks, although this is largely because of mitigations that come for free with closed-source models from OpenAI (e.g., CBRNE classifiers). However, releasing no external safety evals is a bad precedent and gives researchers a false understanding of current risks posed by frontier models. Additionally, if GPT-5.4 Pro turned out to be much better on dual-use tasks (e.g., EVM-Bench or LAB-Bench), we would have been able to update our timelines to the critical period of risk accordingly.&nbsp;</p><h1>This has happened once already</h1><p>The only reason we were tracking this is because I (Parv) accidentally spent $6,000 of Andy’s compute running&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.10362"><u>LAB-Bench</u></a> against GPT-5.2 Pro instead of GPT-5.2 Thinking<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreff2uckcdbpxr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnf2uckcdbpxr">[3]</a></sup></span>, and we noticed quite a high uplift.&nbsp;</p><p><strong><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9woCWqiDbSFPXpxvn/iofcii8wvuol9lm9j5q1" /></strong></p><p>In fact, GPT-5.2 Pro&nbsp;<i>without</i> tools shows comparable performance to&nbsp;<a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/6a5fa276ac68b9aeb0c8b6af5fa36326e0e166dd.pdf#page=35"><u>Opus 4.6&nbsp;</u><i><u>with</u></i><u> tools in Fig-QA (78.3%)</u></a>. We then noticed that we could not corroborate this result, or indeed any safety-relevant benchmark performance, because GPT-5.2 Pro was also released without a system card.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9woCWqiDbSFPXpxvn/jkyiayupkqjcfrwmiyz8" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9woCWqiDbSFPXpxvn/xcjtwhqh47rnbrsk8w4l" /></figure><p>GPT-5.2 Pro was released on December 11, 2025, and Opus 4.6, the first model that seems to outclass it, was released February 5, 2026. Our median guess here is that we had a model that was SOTA on (at minimum) dual-use biology tasks for (at minimum) two months, released without any safety evals, <s>and which the broader safety community largely ignored</s><strong> (see our edit)</strong>.</p><h1>What do we do??</h1><p>We basically assumed that the top three US labs (OAI, Ant, GDM) would, at minimum, publish something matching the concept of a model card with every SOTA model, which was great because it helped us get a better handle on risk. We now think we were wrong, and we can no longer assume labs will provide any safety-relevant benchmark data for their best models at release. However, this data is still extremely important, especially for tracking jagged-y capabilities like CBRNE uplift and cyberoffense.&nbsp;</p><p>At minimum, we recommend that a 1-3 person team at an existing organization:</p><ol><li>Set up a “press the easy button” framework that can run a large eval suite of existing evals as soon as a model is publicly released, and generate a public report describing its potential for catastrophic misuse risk, and give us some insight into whether it’s scheming, prosaically misaligned, etc. To start, this might literally just be ABC-Bench, VCT, Petri, and EVM-Bench<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm0spfy7zht"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm0spfy7zht">[4]</a></sup></span>.</li><li>Run said framework for every major model release without a substantive system card<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4gdsasziiws"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4gdsasziiws">[5]</a></sup></span>.</li></ol><p>We have a list of evaluations we think such a framework should include, and other ideas for how to make this go well—please reach out!</p><p>A more ambitious version of this would also create new evals, and include things like interp to lower-bound sandbagging. It would also coordinate with safety researchers, DC policy folks, and interested parties in USG natsec to frame their assessment in a way accessible to them.&nbsp;</p><p>We are also embarrassed <s>that no one (to our knowledge) has commented on this before</s> <strong>(this is only true for &gt;GPT 5 models; see our edit) </strong>and it took both of us so long to notice this. So, how could we have&nbsp;<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rYq6joCrZ8m62m7ej/how-could-i-have-thought-that-faster"><u>thought this faster</u></a>?</p><ul><li>More people outside labs need to read system cards in full, within days of their release. Set up a reading club, make a really good agent scaffold, find some way of getting the important info into your brain and noticing inconsistencies.<ul><li>Maybe someone can just build a really good Claude Code skill that does this for new model releases? Seems like a task that should take ~2 hours. Get in touch if you’d like to build this!</li></ul></li><li>Lab safety teams should seek through the Frontier Model Forum and other, more informal coordination mechanisms to standardize benchmarks and set a norm of releasing lots of safety benchmark performance data.&nbsp;<ul><li>Safety teams should prioritize releasing safety benchmark datasets, through Trusted Access Programs if appropriate. This will allow the safety community to directly compare different models’ benchmark scores, and get a better sense of risk outside of just “the new one scored less on this sabotage eval.”</li></ul></li></ul><p>In the absence of comprehensive and informative safety evaluations of frontier models from labs themselves, we hope the community can fill this gap while also pushing labs to be more transparent<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflsewcgvj2x"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlsewcgvj2x">[6]</a></sup></span>.&nbsp;</p><p>&nbsp;</p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4m5zkf7jrsx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4m5zkf7jrsx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><s>One question we don't answer here is "what exactly is Pro???" Is it a different model, or a weird scaffold, or finetuning on Thinking, or something else? We don't have great answers here; would love to learn more.</s> <strong>See our edit</strong></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf05a4nkqygr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff05a4nkqygr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Claude has some important notes. “A few caveats worth flagging: the HLE 'with tools' rows use different harnesses (Gemini uses search-blocklist + code; OpenAI's harness isn't specified the same way), so that row is somewhat apples-to-oranges. BrowseComp similarly — Gemini specifies "Search + Python + Browse" while OpenAI's tooling setup isn't detailed identically. GPQA Diamond is essentially a tie at 94.3 vs 94.4."</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf2uckcdbpxr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff2uckcdbpxr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>This was during a forthcoming safety evaluation of Kimi K2.5 with <a href="https://yongzx.github.io/">Yong</a>, aligned with the kind of work we propose above.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm0spfy7zht"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm0spfy7zht">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The main blocker here is cost, but we think funders would be interested in throwing compute at this; we have seen preliminary interest from many stakeholders in the community, in both policy and technical circles.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4gdsasziiws"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4gdsasziiws">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>This would also be extremely useful to get a better handle on risk from Chinese open-source models.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlsewcgvj2x"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflsewcgvj2x">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Huge thanks to everyone on the Kimi K2.5 eval team, without whom we would never have ran into this. We also thank Claude Opus 4.6, who accidentally ran Pro instead of Thinking on LAB-Bench and burnt $6k for what ended up being a good cause. We promise we are competent researchers, and have learnt our lesson.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9woCWqiDbSFPXpxvn/the-current-sota-model-was-released-without-safety-evals#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Can governments quickly and cheaply slow AI training?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/can-governments-quickly-and-cheaply-slow-ai-training)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i>I originally wrote this as a private doc for people working in the field - it's not super polished, or optimized for a broad audience.</i></p><p><i>But I'm publishing anyway because inference-verification is a new and exciting area, and there few birds-eye-view explainers of what's going on and what the bottlenecks are.</i></p><p><strong>Tl;dr:&nbsp;</strong>At least one of the following would need to be implemented for me to be confident that inference verification would substantially slow training given today's algorithms:</p><ol><li>Proof of work or proof of memory that accounts for &gt; 95% of computation.</li><li>Memory wipes every few minutes.</li><li>Output re-computation that reduces covert channel capacity below 0.01%.</li></ol><p>To my knowledge, no one has prototyped verification demos that reach these thresholds; so whether rapidly-implementable inference verification is feasible is still an open question.</p><h1>1. Summary</h1><p>I think powerful AI will be obviously scary at some point, and companies or governments might want to slow it down to buy time for additional safety or oversight. Maybe this could be done quickly, e.g. by:</p><ol><li>Unplugging inter-rack cables to slow gradient syncs</li><li>Limiting bandwidth with simple devices</li><li>Periodically erasing clusters to delete covert training checkpoints</li><li>Recomputing a sample of outputs to confirm they are, in fact, inference generations</li></ol><p>(<u>Section 2</u>)</p><p>Would these methods actually work? Or more specifically, if these methods were implemented quickly and correctly, would they substantially&nbsp;<strong>slow AI development?</strong></p><p>I looked into this question for around a week, and here are my current views:</p><p><strong>Current prototypes of inference-verification would probably be ineffective.&nbsp;</strong>Standard inference-verification measures slow training by&nbsp;<i>restricting communication&nbsp;</i>between servers (see&nbsp;<u>Section 2</u>), since training involves chucking big gradients around in a hivemind, and inference just involves exchanging text. However, communication restrictions might not actually slow AI training much, especially if that training is reinforcement learning (RL).</p><p>RL doesn’t require much communication. A large fraction of RL is&nbsp;<i>inference</i> - for example, generating rollouts of agent actions and scoring them - and inference can still be performed under communication constraints. Developers could use 95% of their compute (the compute that’s under verification) to generate RL rollouts, and 5% of compute in covert data centers to calculate training updates. This might allow training to continue at a similar efficiency as before (<u>Section 3</u>).</p><p>But existing verification proposals would create some hurdles that would make this strategy more difficult. For example, developers would need to frequently upload new checkpoints to inference servers, which could be slow given bandwidth limits. And developers might still need to send some fraction of rollouts through verifier network taps - even though these rollouts don’t actually match expected model outputs. But as I discuss in&nbsp;<u>Section 3.4</u>, these constraints are probably feasible to work around given the slack in&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02620"><u>existing inference verification prototypes</u></a>.</p><p><strong>More aggressive measures could probably buy at least 1 year if implemented sufficiently early.&nbsp;These more aggressive measures include:</strong></p><ol><li><strong>Proof of work or proof of memory that accounts for &gt; 95% of computation, </strong>which would prevent developers from using more than 5% of their compute to secretly generate RL rollouts.</li><li><strong>Memory wipes every few minutes, </strong>which would erase new checkpoints before they can be fully uploaded to inference servers (<u>Section 4</u>).</li><li><strong>Output recomputation that reduces covert channel capacity below 0.01%, </strong>which would prevent developers from&nbsp;<i>exfiltrating&nbsp;</i>covert RL rollout generations through network taps (<u>Section 4</u>).</li></ol><p>Any one of the above would probably make training given current algorithms infeasible. My guess is that developing algorithms to work around these constraints would take at least a year absent rapid AI-driven R&amp;D (if working around them is possible at all).</p><p><strong>A year could be enough.&nbsp;</strong>One year into an AI agreement, governments might have had&nbsp;<i>two years&nbsp;</i>to develop verification in total, since the measures above might require around a year to implement. And after two years of working on verification mechanisms, governments might be able to implement general purpose on-chip security features that are more robust.</p><p><strong>But if an agreement starts during an intelligence explosion, all bets are off.&nbsp;</strong>If developers have powerful AI that can create new algorithms quickly, I see no fundamental reason why bandwidth limits, memory wipes, etc would necessarily slow AI research (<u>Section 5</u>), but they might anyway.</p><p><strong>So I think the main focus of verification research should be on de-risking these three verification measures</strong> (reliable proof of work/memory, frequent memory wipes, and highly accurate output re-computation). Without these measures, I think training would hardly slow at all. With them, training would probably slow down given current algorithms, and &nbsp;would possibly slow down during an intelligence explosion (but that’s unclear).</p><p>None of these measures are obviously feasible to implement on short notice; so I think whether rapidly-retrofitable inference verification is viable is still an open question.</p><p>The next sections defend these claims.</p><p><i>Edit: After thinking about this more and talking to more people, I think developing these measures on short notice is feasible, and a subset of them will probably be prototyped within the next few months. In particular, “proof of memory” and “output re-computation” are probably workable.</i></p><h1>2. Ways to quickly and cheaply slow training by restricting communication</h1><p>During training, models share their learnings in a big hive-mind. There’s a lot of communication going on.</p><p>Here’s what training looks like:</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/hi40hu1qxd8yv6mgqeko" /></p><p>&nbsp;</p><p>During inference, models still talk to each other, but their communications are thin and sparse:</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/p0mt5dfwcec6imunbaar" /></p><p>Could governments exploit this difference in communication to slow down AI training without taxing inference?</p><p>Here’s an overview of different verification methods and how practical I think they are:</p><figure class="table"><table style="border-style: none;"><tbody><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>Method</strong></td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>Can be securely implemented with 1 year of lead time?</strong></td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Remove interconnect</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Yes</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Bandwidth limiters</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Yes (to 100 Kb/s)</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Output recomputation</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Probably, though unclear if covert channels can be reduced below 1% of traffic</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Memory wipes</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Maybe impractical. More than 99.99% of memory has to be erased on a server rack to reliably wipe checkpoints, and this might not be achievable given uncertainties about developer hardware, firmware opaque memory, etc.</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Proof of work / memory</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Probably practical, but has lots of snags, and a robust version hasn’t been demonstrated yet (I will post something on this shortly!)</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>2.1. Method #1: Disconnect inter-rack high-speed cables</h2><p>One way to reduce communication is to&nbsp;<strong>disconnect the high-speed inter-rack network, like InfiniBand cables.&nbsp;</strong>These cables transmit at ~400 GB/s between server racks, and could be replaced with copper connections that only transmit data at internet speeds (e.g. 100 MB/s).</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/l6rqgpgfe0fyd22znofg" /></p><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>2.2. Method #2: Tap-verified bandwidth limits&nbsp;</h2><p>Today’s AI agents don’t need to stream videos. They only need to output text; so developers might&nbsp;<strong>install</strong>&nbsp;<strong>simple devices that tap cables and verify that data transmission rates are even&nbsp;</strong><i><strong>below</strong></i><strong> internet speeds (e.g. 100 KB/s).</strong></p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/c6ztdlodgjtllyad1ffm" /></p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Aside: Why 100 KB/s?</strong> Bandwidth restrictions are most practical to enforce on intra-rack cables - not on the dense copper back-plate connecting GPUs on server racks. How much data needs to flow through these cables to support inference? Server racks currently house around ~100 H100 equivalents. Google had roughly&nbsp;<a href="https://epoch.ai/data/ai-chip-sales"><u>3 million H100 equivalents</u></a> as of late 2025, and processed&nbsp;<a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/ai/io-2025-keynote/"><u>480 trillion text tokens/month</u></a>. This implies 12 billion tokens of input data go through each server rack every second, which is 20 KB/s of input flow (where output flow is probably smaller); so input and output limits of 100 KB/s on each server rack is close to the minimum before data rate limits would interfere with current inference workloads.</p><h2>2.3. Method #3: Output re-computation</h2><p>Verifiers could reduce bandwidth&nbsp;<i>even further&nbsp;</i>by partially recomputing a sample of server outputs<i>.</i></p><ol><li>Developers first declare the program they are running, such as their inference model.</li><li>Then, verifiers record traffic to and from every server with tap-devices, sample input-output pairs, and confirm that the pairs match the behavior of the declared model.</li></ol><p>If this is implemented&nbsp;<i>perfectly - with&nbsp;</i>no leaks and flawless determinism - verifiers know that communications between model instances look like this:</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/skngspgipgdlugc7n9xp" /></p><p>Meaning:</p><ol><li>Models cannot communicate at all with each other inside the server rack. If they did, model outputs wouldn’t be recomputable solely from the inputs.</li><li>Also, cluster outputs can’t be computed from any state covertly stored in the server.</li></ol><p>Model instances can&nbsp;<i>only communicate</i> through low bandwidth model outputs.</p><p>At first glance, this looks like it solves everything. This is close to the most strict communication-constraint that can be enforced without disrupting inference.</p><p>But there are some important caveats.</p><p>First, a developer might be concealing some small fraction of its compute (e.g. 5%) in secret data centers. Historically, intelligence agencies have accounted for covert assets (like tanks, warheads, etc) to within a 15% median error. Compute will probably be easier to account for than (say) missiles. But I think it will still be hard to know if (for example) the PRC government diverted 5% of China’s compute to covert data centers.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/fzqrd8azzw6jamivijsu" /><figcaption><i>&nbsp;Historical intelligence estimates vs ground truth</i></figcaption></figure><p>This gives cheaters a non-zero budget for high-bandwidth operations.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/kmxrdzyudzleukrcbtju" /></p><p>&nbsp;</p><p>Second, output re-computation might not account for&nbsp;<i>all&nbsp;</i>server traffic. There might be non-determinism or side-channels that allow covert workloads on inference servers to leak data.</p><p>So output re-computation might end up functioning as&nbsp;<i>just another way to reduce bandwidth</i>. Suppose covert workloads could only slip data through 1% of output traffic, which&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02620"><u>is the covert channel size that current prototypes allow</u></a>. Then, output bandwidth would be reduced by 100x, from ~100 KB/s to ~1 KB/s.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/r4ann0xix2em7evadg7f" /></p><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>2.4. Method #4: Memory wipes</h2><p>If output re-computation is ‘leaky’ then developers can still accumulate covert state on inference servers. To prevent this state from accumulating for too long, verifiers could&nbsp;<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proof_of_secure_erasure"><i><u>enforce periodic memory wipes</u></i></a><i>.</i> For example:</p><ol><li>Verifiers could require GPUs to generate synthetic random data with some pre-determined seed, such that generating this data is at least&nbsp;<i>a little bit&nbsp;</i>computationally expensive.</li><li>Then, verifiers could ping a random subset of GPUs to ask what data is stored. Since this data is big and time-consuming to generate on the fly, GPUs can only return the data quickly if it is in fact in storage.</li><li>Then, the verifier can compute the data themselves from the shared seed to make sure it’s correct.</li><li>This confirms that the GPUs are in fact storing &gt;99.99% of the synthetic random data developers say they are (if the sample size is sufficiently large).</li><li>If this data accounts for&nbsp;<i>nearly all&nbsp;</i>of the server’s memory, i.e. down to a (1 GB model)/(100 B100s x 192 GB per B100) = 0.005% error - then the verifier knows that the server isn’t storing a covert model.</li><li>Finally, developers re-upload the declared inference model through a high-bandwidth channel, where the declared model is on a white-list and doesn’t count toward bandwidth limits.</li></ol><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/iz6qfbvhvwdiiwko20iv" /></p><h2>2.5. Method #5: Proof of work / proof of memory</h2><p>Verifiers could constrain developers&nbsp;<i><strong>even further&nbsp;</strong></i>with a method called&nbsp;<strong>proof of work / memory.&nbsp;</strong>The idea is to check that&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2504.09971v4"><i>declared inference actually accounts for most of the computation or memory that the developer is using</i></a>. Specifically:</p><ol><li>Verifiers recompute inference outputs (as described) to verify that most output traffic really was generated by the declared inference model.</li><li>Then, verifiers estimate&nbsp;<i>how much total compute&nbsp;</i>or memory these requests would have required to process, e.g. by simply re-running them and recording GPU-hours / GB of memory used.</li><li>Finally, verifiers estimate the&nbsp;<i>compute capacity&nbsp;</i>or memory of the developer’s servers, and check that the vast majority of their compute or memory is accounted for by the declared inference workload.</li></ol><p>Now developers can only use a&nbsp;<i>fraction&nbsp;</i>of their servers for covert workloads - where this fraction is determined by the error bounds of verifier’s estimates.</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/xyukz3se7w5wt4a2uvfo" /></p><h1>3. Ways to efficiently continue training despite these constraints</h1><p>Assume verifiers implement “output re-computation with leaks” from the previous section:</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/n4t6qezgigip2xh7bqye" /></p><p>&nbsp;</p><p>How could developers continue efficient training despite these constraints?</p><p>Here are different methods for communication-efficient training and how effective I think they might be:</p><figure class="table"><table style="border-style: none;"><tbody><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>Method</strong></td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>Bandwidth reduction before performance degrades substantially</strong></td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>Could this technique be used to continue training at near-frontier efficiency despite “output re-computation with leaks”?&nbsp;</strong></td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Larger batch sizes + infrequent SGD steps</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Very little (&lt; 10x)&nbsp;</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">No.</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Periodically merge independent training runs (i.e. infrequent global steps but frequent local ones)&nbsp;</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">At least 1000x, possibly much more.</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Plausibly, even with current algorithms.</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Compress gradients / weights</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">At least 100x.</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Plausibly, with additional R&amp;D.</td></tr><tr><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Use all declared compute for&nbsp; RL rollouts. Do training on dark compute.</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Extreme bandwidth reductions.</td><td style="border-color: #000000; padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Yes, probably, even with current algorithms.</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>3.1. Method #1: Larger batch size + infrequent SGD steps</h2><p><strong>Verdict:&nbsp;</strong>Ineffective.</p><p><strong>Reasoning:&nbsp;</strong>Under 1 kb/s bandwidth, a 10 GB gradient would take 6 months to upload, and many uploads would be required.&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738"><u>Current data</u></a> suggests that the loss landscape is curved enough to where at least thousands of serial training steps are needed to reach strong performance, which implies that a near-frontier training run would take 6 months x 1000 = hundreds of years to complete.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/cydvgaotlmm0vbqbg5ha" /></p><p><i>Caption: The relationship between batch size and number of training steps needed to reach a specific loss (derived from Figure 4 in https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738). Larger batches have no benefit beyond some point (the ‘critical batch size’), and&nbsp;</i><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1KxHZHMU9iFGXzVQFxwRyd6kvldD7gUfNuag3Q15U8jU/edit?tab=t.0#bookmark=id.yld9502m21r"><i><u>this BOTEC</u></i></a><i> suggests we are already near this point.</i></p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/cag1rkbxdnhdvgysttvx" /></p><p><i>Caption: The “critical batch size” is increasing more slowly than developers are scaling AI training, which suggests that developers will remain in a regime where increasing the batch size doesn’t reduce the number of syncs required. From:&nbsp;</i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08105"><i><u>https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08105</u></i></a></p><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>3.2. Method #2: Periodically merge independent training runs&nbsp;</h2><p><strong>Verdict:&nbsp;</strong>Reduces bandwidth requirements by at least 1000x, and possibly much more.</p><p><strong>Reasoning:&nbsp;</strong>Merging independently trained models reduces loss because the loss landscape is convex; so averaging models achieves lower than the average of the models’ losses.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/xiiosr6m2u2n4hr5cloa" /></p><p><i>Caption: Why merging independently trained models works.</i></p><p>Google shows that this technique can reduce the frequency of gradient syncs by ~2000x with minimal hits to performance (see&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.18512"><u>https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.18512</u></a> and&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08105"><u>https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08105</u></a>):</p><p>&nbsp;<img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/y1uuxrhly1ofra0jniba" /></p><p><i>Caption: Training loss for different sync frequencies</i>. From the&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08105"><u>DiLoCo paper</u></a>.</p><p>However, beyond a certain point, reducing syncing frequency&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.13761"><u>starts to harm performance</u></a>. But&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.03306"><u>Li et al</u></a> showed that this problem can be mitigated by training models on different and independent subsets of data. For example, a model that’s learning pytorch does not need to sync frequently with a model learning about game development. The pytorch model’s learnings don’t have serial dependencies with the game development model’s learnings; so training can be parallelized to the extent that the data can be fragmented into isolated domains.</p><p>However, models&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.16298"><u>benefit non-trivially from generalization</u></a>; so at what sync frequencies do performance costs kick? One paper trained experts with&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.07816"><u>~10,000 H100 hours</u></a> before merging at high performance; so if we assume that each server trains a single model, and a server has 100 GPUs, then developers could sync models every 10,000 / 100 / 24 = 4 days and maintain frontier efficiency.</p><h2>3.3. Method #3: Compress gradients and weights</h2><p><strong>Verdict:&nbsp;</strong>Reduces bandwidth requirements by at least ~100 times, and possibly much more.</p><p><strong>Reasoning:&nbsp;</strong>Developers can reduce the size of (1) gradients (2) pseudo gradients and (3) model weights, which reduces the time required to send these objects faster to share across bandwidth-limited channels.</p><ol><li><strong>Gradients</strong> can be compressed because they are&nbsp;<a href="http://papers.neurips.cc/paper/7697-sparsified-sgd-with-memory.pdf"><u>sparse</u></a> (mostly zeros) and&nbsp;<a href="https://www.semanticscholar.org/reader/b4a632a7097e7d0631250884dfc6e1f76b376996"><u>low-rank</u></a>. Compression can reduce the size of gradients by around 100x with&nbsp;<a href="http://papers.neurips.cc/paper/7697-sparsified-sgd-with-memory.pdf"><u>minimal</u></a>&nbsp;<a href="https://www.semanticscholar.org/reader/b4a632a7097e7d0631250884dfc6e1f76b376996"><u>performance decline</u></a>.</li><li>Developers can also compress&nbsp;<i><strong>pseudo gradients</strong>&nbsp;</i>- which are the model deltas from training models independently (as discussed in the previous section). Pseudo gradients can be compressed by around&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15706"><u>100x</u></a> as well.</li><li>Finally, developers can make&nbsp;<strong>model weights</strong> small and easy to communicate. One way to do this is to split model weights into many tiny experts, each of which is hosted on a single server; so the time needed to upload each expert through bandwidth limits is short.&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.03529"><u>This paper</u></a> splits a model into 32 fully-independent experts, so a 100 GB model might be divided into ~3 GB experts with negligible hit to performance.</li></ol><p>In the limit, how much can gradients be compressed without slowing down training? Theoretically: there’s no free lunch. At a certain threshold, compressing by 10x slows training by 10x. But what is this threshold? In principle, gradients could be losslessly compressed to the size of the training data used to generate them. If training data is ~1 million times smaller than a gradient (~100 GB / 100 KB), then gradients could be compressed by ~1 million times. Also, training data itself is<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.04564"><u> fairly compressible</u></a>, so gradients might be compressible by&nbsp;<i>even more&nbsp;</i>than 1 million times without any hit to performance. Gradients are not&nbsp;<i>currently&nbsp;</i>this compressible, but maybe developers could make them&nbsp;<i>more compressible&nbsp;</i>by “<a href="https://openreview.net/pdf/80c3726eabbc5235709ba9721f18a8368b758d79.pdf"><u>training for compressibility</u></a>,” or other methods.</p><h2>3.4. Method #4: Use more compute for inference rollouts, and less for training</h2><p>Most of RL training involves generating action rollouts and scoring them. This is just inference, so developers could use their declared inference servers for rollout generation, and use secret data centers for training.&nbsp;</p><p>If secret data centers are&nbsp;<a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1BsDPQWiohlM2bbF-QE04F1hki0Z3KFjmOG7eiJ7K11c/edit?tab=t.pci0brir8qqg#bookmark=id.6h36sn633bqi"><u>~5% of developer</u></a>, then developers could use this 5% for training, and 95% for rollout generation. Would this asymmetric compute allocation slow down RL training?&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.13818"><u>Multiple</u></a>&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.14338"><u>papers</u></a> suggest no: the vast majority of rollouts are not very informative. So if developers train on only the most informative 5% of rollouts, RL still converges at a similar rate. [And also,&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24372"><u>maybe gradients aren’t even necessary at all, and inference-based evolution works really well</u></a>]</p><p>However, this strategy still requires surmounting a couple of challenges:</p><p><strong>First, new checkpoints have to be periodically uploaded to inference servers.&nbsp;</strong>RL generates rollouts&nbsp;<i>using the model being trained</i>. So, new models would need to be uploaded to inference servers continually during training. But bandwidth limits could make this upload process slow.</p><p>At 100 KB/s, a 3 GB expert model could be uploaded every few hours. Currently, models during reinforcement learning are updated every ~few minutes (the time needed to generate rollouts); so this would be a ~100x slower update frequency than is typical.</p><p>However, model updates are becoming less frequent by default because rollouts are taking longer to generate. Also, updates can be&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.19362"><u>significantly delayed</u></a> without harming performance; so an update frequency of a few hours probably is not a problem.</p><p><strong>Additionally, the rollout data has to sent through verifier taps even though it isn't generated by declared models.&nbsp;</strong>We’re assuming that verifiers use output re-computation to verify that outputs are generated by the “declared” inference model. But the model producing rollouts for RL is different from the “declared model” and is constantly changing; so RL rollouts would need to somehow escape servers even though they can’t be re-computed by verifiers.</p><p>Developers might exfiltrate RL rollouts via the&nbsp;<i>non-determinism&nbsp;</i>of inference. Output re-computation naturally has some error, since inference is not&nbsp;<i>perfectly&nbsp;</i>deterministic. Even hardware-level irregularities will cause some deviations. In <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02620">current prototypes</a>, the “unsuspicious error” (aka level of non-determinism) of output re-computation is 1%; so 1% of server outputs could be generated by our undeclared RL model without raising suspicion.</p><p>If only around 1% of the RL rollouts generated could be actually sent back to training servers, is this a big constraint? Servers would need to curate the top&nbsp;<i>5%&nbsp;</i>of rollouts anyway, since this is the maximum the training clusters can digest (remember that we are assuming covert data centers are 5% of total developer compute); so further tightening the curation threshold to the top 1% probably wouldn’t be a big problem.</p><p>Therefore, developers could potentially side-step inference verification by using declared servers for RL rollout generation.</p><h1>4. But more aggressive verification methods would probably make training with current algorithms impractical</h1><p>If we combine the most promising communication-efficient training methods from the last section, here’s what developers might do:</p><ol><li>Developers might use all of their declared servers to generate RL rollouts, and use their unreported compute for AI training.</li><li>They might curate RL rollouts to the top 1%, exfiltrate this small fraction through inference noise.</li><li>Finally, developers might upload new checkpoints periodically to inference servers by transmitting ~3 GB expert models.</li></ol><p>To defeat this strategy, verifiers could implement any of the following:</p><ol><li><strong>Proof of work or proof of memory that accounts for &gt; 95% of computation.&nbsp;</strong>If verifiers can confirm that &gt;95% of compute in inference servers is being used to serve the declared model, then developers can only use &lt; 5% of compute to covertly generate RL rollouts. ~5% is the important threshold because, below this point, developers don’t gain much from secret inference relative to running inference on the covert data centers that already might represent around 5% of their compute.</li><li><p><strong>Memory wipes every few minutes.</strong>&nbsp;Each time verifiers wipe servers, developers need to re-upload the 3 GB expert models. But uploading 3 GB across 100 KB/s would take 8 hours; so if memory is wiped every few minutes, developers couldn’t upload a full expert before all of their progress is erased.</p><p>In order to upload a checkpoint within the 1-minute memory-wipe window, models experts would need to be around ~5 MB. I’m not aware of any work that has split a near-frontier, 100 GB model into 20,000 different independent 5 MB experts and retained high performance. I’m not sure if this is even possible.</p></li><li><strong>Output re-computation that reduces covert channel capacity below 0.01%.&nbsp;</strong>The performance of RL already starts to degrade when down-sampling to 1/16th of rollout data (see figure 4 of&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.13818"><u>https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.13818</u></a>). If output recomputation has a covert channel capacity of &lt; 0.01%, then rollouts would need to be down-sampled by 10,000x to exfiltrate them through inference noise. This is much more extreme down-sampling than what I’ve seen in any published RL implementation.</li></ol><p>Any of the above inference verification methods would probably significantly slow training&nbsp;<i>given</i>&nbsp;<i>current algorithms</i>.</p><h1>5. However, if developers (or AIs) have a lot of time to research better algorithms, all bets are off</h1><p>All of the inference verification methods above boil down to&nbsp;<i>restricting communications between servers</i>. This only works because&nbsp;<i>training currently involves a lot more communication than inference does.&nbsp;</i>But I see no reason why this&nbsp;<i>has&nbsp;</i>to be the case.</p><p>For example:</p><ol><li><strong>Frequent memory wipes could be defeated by training&nbsp;</strong><i><strong>thousands of tiny experts</strong>,&nbsp;</i>This would allow tiny 10 MB experts to be quickly uploaded before memory wipes erase progress. I see no strong argument that models&nbsp;<i>can’t&nbsp;</i>be subdivided into many 10 MB experts.</li><li><strong>Bandwidth limits can be defeated with extreme gradient compression.</strong> In principle, gradients could be compressed to sizes even smaller than the data that generated them. For example, consider how compressible&nbsp;<i>human learning&nbsp;</i>is. I could trip on my shoelaces, and the whole experience would require a lot of storage to record on video. But the lesson I draw would simply be the words “tie your shoe next time.” Maybe models could share small fragments of information that capture most of the value of a gradient update.</li><li><strong>Bandwidth limits can be defeated with infrequent syncs.&nbsp;</strong>The core reason communication is important during training is that many domains have&nbsp;<i>serial dependencies</i>: one skill needs to be learned before another. But no one knows exactly how strong these dependencies are. Serial dependencies might only be strongest within a particular subdomain (like “coding in pytorch”) and might be&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.03306"><u>very weak</u></a>&nbsp;<i>between different&nbsp;</i>subdomains; so AI agents could train thousands of subdomain experts in series, on small, isolated servers, and merge them infrequently (e.g. every few weeks), and still&nbsp;<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08105"><u>retain most of the performance of normal training</u></a>.</li><li><strong>Even if “frontier training” is slow, AI research might not be.&nbsp;</strong>Developers might still make rapid algorithmic progress by training thousands of small models in parallel, e.g. one ~gpt-3 sized model on every server-rack of ~100 GPUs, every few hours. Then, developers would only need to transfer&nbsp;<i>experiment results&nbsp;</i>through bandwidth limits, rather than gradients. Maybe&nbsp;<i>training lots of small models&nbsp;</i>is a good way to improve AI software.</li></ol><p>All of these are plausible reasons why even&nbsp;<i>extreme&nbsp;</i>communication restrictions&nbsp;<i>might not slow AI development much at all.&nbsp;</i>However, I think it’s also plausible that communication constraints would cause a major slowdown - even in the limit of AI R&amp;D. This all just depends on the&nbsp;<i>nature&nbsp;</i>of AI development and learning. AI development might be intrinsically much more bottlenecked on compute than communication. Or it might not be. We don’t know.</p><h1>6. Conclusion</h1><p>More work is needed to be confident that inference verification can be effectively implemented on short notice.</p><p>If you are interested in doing this work, say so, and I can try to connect you to the relevant people.</p><p>The best way to reach me is via email: joshuamclymer@gmail.com&nbsp;</p><h1>Appendix</h1><h2>Are we in the serially bottlenecked training regime? A BOTEC by Claude</h2><h2><strong>Setup</strong></h2><p>There is a critical batch size (B_crit) beyond which adding more data-parallel workers yields diminishing returns. Below B_crit, doubling the batch size roughly halves the number of training steps needed — perfect parallelization. Above B_crit, you still need roughly the same number of serial steps, but you're burning extra tokens for no benefit.</p><p>If a training cluster can fill B_crit with its data-parallel capacity, it is&nbsp;<strong>serial-bottlenecked</strong> — more GPUs won't help. If it can't reach B_crit, it is&nbsp;<strong>hardware-bottlenecked</strong> — more GPUs would directly speed up training.</p><p>This BOTEC asks: at the scale of 100K and 1M H100-equivalents, which regime are we in?</p><h2><strong>Key Formula</strong></h2><p>From Bergsma et al. 2025 ("Power Lines"), trained on GPT-2-style models up to 3.3B parameters on SlimPajama:</p><p></p><p>where B is in sequences of 2048 tokens and D is total training tokens. This was fit on datasets up to ~143B tokens; we are extrapolating 100–400× beyond the fitted range.</p><h2><strong>B_crit at Frontier Scale</strong></h2><figure class="table"><table style="border-style: none;"><tbody><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>Dataset size (D)</strong></p></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>B_crit (tokens/batch)</strong></p></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>S_min (steps)</strong></p></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><p><strong>Wall-clock at B_crit, 2s/step</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">15T (DeepSeek-V3 scale)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">118M</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">127K</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">5.9 days</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">30T (Llama 4 scale)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">162M</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">185K</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">8.5 days</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">60T (next-gen frontier)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">224M</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">268K</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">12.4 days</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>At B_crit, the number of training steps is 2 × S_min, and the total tokens consumed is 2 × D_min. The lab pays a 2× token overhead in exchange for minimizing wall-clock time.</p><h2><strong>How Many GPUs Per Model Replica?</strong></h2><p>Different architectures consume vastly different amounts of model parallelism, leaving different amounts of headroom for data parallelism:</p><figure class="table"><table style="border-style: none;"><tbody><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Dense ~600B</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">8</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">32</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">—</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">256</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">MoE 671B (DeepSeek-V3 style)</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">1</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">16</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>1M</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">MoE 671B</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">976</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">64M tok</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">0.5×</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Hardware-bottlenecked</td></tr><tr><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;"><strong>1M</strong></td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">MoE ~2T</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">244</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">16M tok</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">0.1×</td><td style="padding: 5pt; vertical-align: top;">Hardware-bottlenecked</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h2><strong>Key Takeaways</strong></h2><p><strong>At 100K H100s, every architecture is hardware-bottlenecked.</strong> Even a relatively small dense model can only reach ~0.4× B_crit. More GPUs would directly speed up training. MoE models are especially far from B_crit because expert parallelism consumes most of the GPU budget.</p><p><strong>At 1M H100s, dense models become serial-bottlenecked but MoE models do not.</strong> A dense 300B model would overshoot B_crit by 4×, wasting significant compute on redundant gradient information. But a DeepSeek-V3-style MoE still only reaches 0.5× B_crit, and a 2T-parameter MoE reaches just 0.1×. MoE architectures absorb GPU capacity into model parallelism, keeping the data-parallel dimension small and the training compute-efficient.</p><p><strong>This is a structural argument for MoE at scale.</strong> As clusters grow, dense models hit the seriality wall first. MoE provides a way to productively use additional GPUs (holding more expert parameters) without pushing batch size past B_crit. It converts excess parallel capacity into model quality rather than wasted data parallelism.</p><p><strong>If the Power Lines extrapolation holds, serial wall-clock time is surprisingly short.</strong> At B_crit with 2s/step, a 15T-token run finishes in ~6 days. Actual frontier training runs take months, suggesting labs operate well below B_crit — trading wall-clock time for compute efficiency — or that step times are much longer than 2 seconds at these configurations.</p><h2><strong>Caveats</strong></h2><p>These estimates rest on several shaky assumptions:</p><ol><li><strong>Extrapolation.</strong> The Power Lines scaling law was fit on models ≤3.3B parameters and datasets ≤143B tokens. Extrapolating to 15–60T tokens is a 100–400× extrapolation. The exponent (0.462) could be different at frontier scale.<br /><br />&nbsp;</li><li><strong>MoE.</strong> The scaling law was fit on dense models. MoE architectures may have different B_crit scaling — the gradient noise structure could differ when only a fraction of parameters are active per token. No published work has measured B_crit for large MoE models.<br /><br />&nbsp;</li><li><strong>Parallelism overhead.</strong> The model parallelism estimates (TP, PP, EP) are rough. Real configurations depend on interconnect topology, memory capacity, and engineering choices. Some labs may achieve higher DP with clever parallelism strategies.<br /><br />&nbsp;</li><li><strong>Step time.</strong> We assumed 2 seconds per step, which is a rough estimate. At high DP with large models, communication overhead can push step times to 5–10+ seconds, significantly increasing wall-clock time.<br /><br />&nbsp;</li><li><strong>Batch size warmup.</strong> B_crit is not constant during training — it starts near zero and grows. Early training is always highly serial regardless of cluster size.<br /><br />&nbsp;</li></ol><h2><strong>Sources</strong></h2><ul><li>Bergsma et al. 2025, "Power Lines: Scaling Laws for Large Language Model Training" (arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738)</li><li>McCandlish et al. 2018, "An Empirical Model of Large-Batch Training" (arxiv.org/abs/1812.06162)</li><li>Merrill et al. 2025, "Critical Batch Size Revisited" (arxiv.org/abs/2505.23971)</li><li>Epoch AI, "Data Movement Bottlenecks: Scaling Past 1e28 FLOP" (epoch.ai/blog/data-movement-bottlenecks-scaling-past-1e28-flop)</li><li>DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report (arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437)</li></ul><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xzf3eMnhTko7AxnEy/can-governments-quickly-and-cheaply-slow-ai-training#comments">Discuss</a>

---

**摘要**: <p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> has long been uncomfortable with software
      patents. But a direct experience of patent aggression, together with the
      practical constraints faced by startups, led him to resort to defensive
      patenting as as a shield in this asymmetric legal environment.</p>

### [Humans and Agents in Software Engineering Loops](https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/humans-and-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/humans-and-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>There's been much talk recently about how AI agents affect the workflow
      loops of software development. <b class="author">Kief Morris</b> believes the
      answer is to focus on the goal of turning ideas into outcomes. The right
      place for us humans is to build and manage the working loop rather than
      either leaving the agents to it or micromanaging what they produce.</p>

### [Design-First Collaboration](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/design-first-collaboration.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><b class="author">Rahul Garg</b> continues his series of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/">Patterns for Reducing Friction in
      AI-Assisted Development</a>. This pattern describes <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/design-first-collaboration.html">a structured
      conversation</a> that mirrors whiteboarding with a human pair: progressive
      levels of design alignment before any code, reducing cognitive load, and
      catching misunderstandings at the cheapest possible moment. </p>

### [Fragments: February 25](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-02-25.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I don’t tend to post links to videos here, as <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AvoidingVideo.html">I can’t stand watching videos to learn about things</a>. But some talks are worth a watch, and I do suggest this overview on <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LOHgRw43fFk">how organizations are currently using AI</a> by Laura Tacho. There’s various nuggets of data from her work with DX:</p>

<ul>
  <li>92.6% of devs are using AI assistants</li>
  <li>devs reckon it’s saving them 4 hours per week</li>
  <li>27% of code is written by AI without significant human intervention</li>
  <li>AI cuts onboarding time by half</li>
</ul>

<p>These are interesting numbers, but most of them are averages, and those who know me know I teach people to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/dont-compare-averages.html">be suspicious of averages</a>. Laura knows this too:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>average doesn’t mean typical.. there is no typical experience with AI</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Different companies (and teams within companies) are having very different experiences. Often AI is an amplifier to an organization’s practices, for good or ill.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Organizational performance is multidimensional, and these organizations are
just going off into different extremes based on what they were doing before. AI
is an accelerator, it’s a multiplier, and it is moving organizations off in
different directions.
(08:52)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Some organizations are facing twice as many customer incidents, but others are facing half.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Rachel Laycock (Thoughtworks CTO) <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/articles/reflections-future-software-engineering-retreat">shares her reflections</a> on our recent Future of Software Engineering retreat in Utah.</p>

<ul>
  <li>We need to address cognitive load</li>
  <li>The staff engineer role is changing</li>
  <li>What happens to code reviews?</li>
  <li>Agent Topologies</li>
  <li>What exactly does AI mean for programming languages?</li>
  <li>Self-healing systems</li>
</ul>

<p>On the latter:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One of the most interesting and perhaps immediately applicable ideas was the concept of an ‘agent subconscious’, in which agents are informed by a comprehensive knowledge graph of post mortems and incident data. This particularly excites me because I’ve seen many production issues solved by the latent knowledge of those in leadership positions. The constant challenge comes from what happens when those people aren’t available or involved.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Simon Willison (one of my most reliable sources for information about LLMs and programming) is starting a series of <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Feb/23/agentic-engineering-patterns/">Agentic Engineering Patterns</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I think of vibe coding using its original definition of coding where you pay no attention to the code at all, which today is often associated with non-programmers using LLMs to write code.</p>

  <p>Agentic Engineering represents the other end of the scale: professional software engineers using coding agents to improve and accelerate their work by amplifying their existing expertise.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He’s intending this to be closer to evergreen material, as opposed to the day-to-day writing he does (extremely well) on his blog.</p>

<p>One of the first patterns is  <a href="https://simonwillison.net/guides/agentic-engineering-patterns/red-green-tdd/">Red/Green TDD</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This turns out to be a fantastic fit for coding agents. A significant risk with coding agents is that they might write code that doesn’t work, or build code that is unnecessary and never gets used, or both.</p>

  <p>Test-first development helps protect against both of these common mistakes, and also ensures a robust automated test suite that protects against future regressions.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/AaronErickson/status/2026146310840332332">Aaron Erickson</a> is one of those technologists with good judgment who I listen to a lot</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>As much fun as people are having with OpenClaw, I think the days of “here is my agent with access to all my stuff” are numbered.</p>

  <p>Fine scoped agents who can read email and cleanse it before it reaches the agentic OODA loop that acts on it, policy agents (a claw with a job called “VP of NO” to money being spent)</p>

  <p>You structure your agents like you would a company. Insert friction where you want decisions to be slow and the cost of being wrong is high, reduce friction where you want decisions to be fast and the cost of being wrong is trivial or zero.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I’ve posted here a lot about security concerns with agents. Right now I think this notion of fine-scoped agents is the most promising direction. Last year Korny Sietsma <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#split-tasks">wrote about how to mitigate agentic AI security risks</a>. His advice included to split the tasks,  so that no agent has access to all parts of the Lethal Trifecta:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This approach is an application of a more general security habit: follow the Principle of Least Privilege. Splitting the work, and giving each sub-task a minimum of privilege, reduces the scope for a rogue LLM to cause problems, just as we would do when working with corruptible humans.</p>

  <p>This is not only more secure, it is also increasingly a way people are encouraged to work. It’s too big a topic to cover here, but it’s a good idea to split LLM work into small stages, as the LLM works much better when its context isn’t too big. Dividing your tasks into “Think, Research, Plan, Act” keeps context down, especially if “Act” can be chunked into a number of small independent and testable chunks.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Doonesbury outlines the <a href="https://x.com/RealRBHJr/status/2025590410018484473/photo/1">opportunity for aging writers like myself</a>. (Currently I’m still writing my words the old fashioned way.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>An interesting story someone told me. They were at a swimming pool with their child, she looked at a photo on a poster advertising an event there and said “that’s AI”. Initially the parents didn’t think it was, but looking carefully spotted a tell-tale six fingers. They concluded that fresher biological neural networks are being trained to quickly recognize AI.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I carefully curate my social media streams, following only feeds where I can control whose posts are picked up. In times gone by, editors of newspapers and magazines would do a similar job. But many users of social media <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2026/feb/23/15-year-old-girl-misogyny-social-media-online-abuse">are faced with a tsunami</a> of stuff, much of it ugly, and don’t have to tools to control it.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few days ago I saw an Instagram reel of a young woman talking about how she had been raped six years ago, struggled with thoughts of suicide afterwards, but managed to rebuild her life again. Among the comments – the majority of which were from men – were things like “Well at least you had some”, “No way, she’s unrapeable”, “Hope you didn’t talk this much when it happened”, “Bro could have picked a better option.” Reading those comments, which had thousands of likes and many boys agreeing with them, made me feel sick.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>My tendencies are to free speech, and I try not to be a Free Speech Poseur, but the deluge of ugly material on the internet isn’t getting any better. The people running these platforms seem to be “tackling” this problem by putting their heads in the sand and hoping it won’t hurt them. It is hurting their users.</p>

### [Knowledge Priming](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/knowledge-priming.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><b class="author">Rahul Garg</b> has observed a frustration loop when
      working with AI coding assistants - lots of code generated, but needs lots

      patterns</a> that help improve the interaction with the LLM, and describes
      the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/knowledge-priming.html">first of these</a>: priming the LLM with knowledge about the codebase and
      preferred coding patterns.</p>
